Initial hypothesis: a function defined by a Dirichlet series and a Gamma-type functional equation might violate Average Abscissa Law. Davenport-Heilbronn functions are now under scrutiny due to their non-Euler product structure, requiring investigation into whether they exhibit AAL violations. Analysis now centers on Epstein zeta functions within quadratic fields of class number 3, given their relation with Hecke L-functions and Dirichlet L-functions, focusing on the potential for Average Abscissa Law (AAL) violations.

The core question has emerged: does a Dirichlet series function with a Gamma functional equation, and meromorphic extension, necessarily satisfy Average Abscissa Law (AAL)? The critical line contains finitely many singularities of G, not cancelled by Φ. Φ has simple poles at points in a finite set P. The difference Φ - Σ_p r_p/(s-p) is holomorphic and bounded on the strip near the line. The two series differ by the polar part, Σ b_i {a_i u} - Σ c_j {q_j u} = Σ_p r_p u^p. The polar part is continuous; jumps and merged weights agree at every u₀ > 0 and scale. It follows that the two families are equal.

The presence of finitely many poles on the critical line doesn't affect the jump condition because the inverse Mellin transform of a finite sum of poles results in a smooth function. This smoothness contrasts with the jumps in the inverse Mellin transform of the zeta function-related expansion, thus ensuring that finitely many poles on the line have zero contribution to the jump at all u₀ > 0. This concept applies directly to World I, at the line defined by the real part of s = Θ, where Θ is between 1/2 and 1 or 1-Θ between 0 and 1/2.

The jump condition must be considered on both sides of the line Re(s) = Θ. Examining the right side (Re(s) > Θ) reveals that -ζ'(s)/ζ(s) does not have a simple pole at s=1. However, absolute convergence for this expression only holds for Re(s) > 1, not for Θ < Re(s) ≤ 1. The previous proof required absolute convergence.

The Mellin transform of -ζ(s)/s is the sawtooth function, which has unit jumps at every positive integer and is smooth elsewhere. Previously hypothesized that a continuous function was involved but it now appears the Mellin inverse for Fake A shares the same jumps as the sawtooth function. Analyzing Fake A reveals the difference between -F_A(s)/s and -ζ_RH(s)/s is in L^1. Thus, the Mellin inverse will be continuous and the jumps are identical.

The observation from TheJumpLawOnAStripSurvivesFinitelyManyPoles.lean line 19 confirms that the polar part's continuity ensures jump agreement at all positive u₀. This means L^1 perturbations on a vertical line, such as multiplying by an appropriate function, introduces only continuous components in u-space. Therefore, jumps alone cannot differentiate the target function from Fake A. The key insight is that because the difference between Fake A and the target function is in L^1, its Mellin inverse is continuous. This continuity explains why their jumps match.

The Mellin transform of Fake A is not a Dirichlet series; its corresponding Perron step function is nonzero on (0,1) and non-linear between integers, unlike standard Dirichlet series. This is a key distinguishing factor, as standard Dirichlet series exhibit specific properties on the real part greater than 1, including Bohr almost periodicity, which Fake A lacks. The focus shifts to analyzing how Bohr almost periodicity relates to the behavior of the function on the real part greater than 1.

The difference between F_A and the Riemann zeta function approaches zero as the imaginary part of the complex variable tends to infinity. Applying the property of a Bohr almost periodic function converging to zero at infinity, it must be identically zero. The Riemann zeta function, even under the Riemann Hypothesis, is not uniformly Bohr almost periodic for the real part greater than one-half, due to unboundedness. However, it is Besicovitch B² almost periodic for the real part greater than one-half. Specifically, the Riemann zeta function, its reciprocal, and related functions are uniformly Bohr almost periodic where the real part is greater than or equal to 1 + η or less than or equal to -η.

The key distinction is that on vertical lines with a negative real part, the target function exhibits Bohr almost periodicity, while "Fake A" does not. The review explicitly frames the question of whether the supremum of the real parts of zeta zeros is attained off the critical line (AAL) as a key open problem within this corpus, as of the most recent report. The review details the relationship between AAL and related conjectures and functions, and notes that AAL is still open (unproven/unrefuted). The next step is to examine this open question from the perspective of how the corpus presents the problem.

The core of the problem is determining the behavior of the smoothed prime error under different conditions, particularly if it tends to a non-zero finite trigonometric polynomial (World I) or tends to zero (World II). The Almost All Zeros (AAL) conjecture posits that the intermediate case, where this error is neither a finite trigonometric polynomial nor zero, does not occur; if 
Θ

Θ>1/2, the smoothed prime error is o(x^Θ). The goal is to articulate the distinct nature of AAL compared to the Riemann Hypothesis (RH) and Quasi-RH by establishing that proving RH or Quasi-RH requires ruling out infinite families of zeros at unbounded heights (World II), where no finite polynomial annihilates the top zeros and no Backlund–Lindelöf order-0 strip exists. AAL focuses on whether a specific intermediate case exists that violates a strong bound on the smoothed prime error.

Attempting to synthesize the prior failures, the core issue appears to be a consistent neglect of the implications of finite height. Previous methods, specifically those relying solely on analytic, meromorphic properties or Dirichlet series, failed to leverage the constraint that zeros are finite in number and height within a specific region (World I assumption). The blind spot was the lack of application of a global argument. All fourteen failed attempts did not enforce the finite number of zeros.

Both "Fake A" and "Fake AB" lack the Nyman-Beurling representation present in the real Riemann zeta function. This representation is tied to the exact step function and the functional equation, allowing it to be expressed via Mellin integral. The absence of this property suggests a fundamental difference in the structure and behavior of these "fake" functions compared to the genuine zeta function.

"Fake A" fails to maintain modular invariance within an Eisenstein series, stemming from the modification's disruption of required symmetry. This prevents the function from possessing an Euler product or satisfying any standard divisor identities, additive characters, Dirichlet characters, or modularity properties found in classical Dirichlet series. The failure to exhibit these fundamental characteristics eliminates it as a viable counterexample.

The analysis highlights the critical failure points of "Fake A" and "Fake AB" functions. Specifically, neither function supports the key properties required for either the Asymptotic Abscissa Law or a counterexample to the Riemann Hypothesis. "Fake A" fails the necessary conditions for Dirichlet 
𝐿
L-functions and modularity because it cannot generate an Euler product. "Fake AB" fails to produce the structure of an Eisenstein series due to disrupted symmetry. Both fail the property of exact integrality. These shortcomings invalidate them as valid mathematical objects to consider for the proof.
